Dear lawmakers, budgetary rights are the decision of the nation itself to determine the extent and size of the state's expenditures, and how much to spend, and how much to fund these expenditures. In democracies, the nation makes this decision through elected representatives and by drafting budget laws. In Turkey, lawmakers in Turkey's Grand National Assembly, as representatives of the nation, express the budget rights of the nation through the votes here, through their views, by drafting budget laws. Governments in democracies are allowed by budget laws to collect taxes from parliament, authorise spending. It is, of course, the right of the nation to monitor whether this tax collection permit and the authority to spend are in accordance with its purpose and purpose. Budget rights should not end in budgeting, but should also be supervised and known about the results of this budget implementation. Turkey's foreign debt rose from 129 billion to 367 billion dollars, which is not bearable. When the household is in debt like this, the debts are not paid by income, but by wealth, by deed, by means of property. In the process, as always, we will keep very close watch and remain cautious. Precious lawmakers also need to consider how sustainable growth is, the expected moderate recovery for the coming term, while the projections of growth on the world economy are being revised downwards. In this framework, the quality of growth in the coming period will be very, very important for many countries. What do we mean by the quality of growth? Can't we say that both countries have grown by 3%, that these two countries are equally successful? That's where we need a good analysis. There are three key factors for what we call the quality of growth; one is financial sustainability, the other is social sustainability, and the third is environmental sustainability. What do we mean by financial sustainability? Growth has been achieved, but has the debt of that country's treasure increased or has it fallen on a reasonable level? Growth has been achieved, but did that country's Central Bank make an extraordinary amount of money for free, or did the Central Bank continue with reasonable policies? Growth has been achieved, but to what extent did the debt of the citizens of that country come, and to what extent did the debt of the corporations of that country come? This is all very critical. If these financial elements are not assessed together and ignored by growth, the next stage can be a major obstacle to growth. The second issue is social sustainability. Again, think of two countries, both of which have grown by 3%. Has this growth created employment? Has growth helped close the gap between the rich and the poor? Or did he grow up to be richer, richer, poorer, poorer? Is the welfare that comes from growth fairly shared, or does growth also mean the increase in income? There's no doubt that you need to look at these. There is growth, but is growth useful for the education system of that country, is it moving that country's healthcare system forward, or is it just getting more waste, more consumption, more luxury in the hands of a certain population? As we look at the growth of countries, we must not only pay attention to numbers, but also to the social consequences of that growth. The third important issue is the environmental sustainability of growth. Imagine if a country was growing 56%, but this growth was associated with terrible environmental destruction? Carbon monoxide emissions, the rapid destruction of forests, the rapid disappearance of clean water supplies, does that bring about growth? Maybe that's how you make growth, you grow up today, but you grow up stealing the prosperity and quality of life from future generations. So, this is all the stuff that the world needs to think about a lot more in the coming period. Of course, this financial crisis, 2008, 2009, and what we've experienced in so many countries has shaken governments so much, it's so frightening that we don't have to look at where it's coming from, where it's coming from, how it's coming from now. There's a superficial approach like this. In fact, the work is going around, the area we call the economy is a human-focused area, a human-based area, and its goal is human again. No economic policy is successful in the long run that comes from humans and doesn't focus on people. I talked about the third issue, environmental sustainability. When we look at the whole picture, these three elements, a country can only continue to grow in a sustainable, healthy way, with an economic policy that deals with the financial size, social size, and environment together in an integrated way.
